                                                    150
13      Spontaneous symmetry breaking and the Higgs mechanism
So far we have mostly only considered global symmetries that leave the ground state invariant. In general
quantum systems this is by no means the only possibility, and a global symmetry which does not leave the
ground state invariant is said to be spontaneously broken. There is also an analogue of spontaneous
symmetry breaking for gauge symmetries, called the Higgs mechanism, although in that case the ground
state is still unique. In this section we will learn some basics about both of these phenomena.

13.1      Spontaneous symmetry breaking in quantum mechanics
A rather trivial example of spontaneous symmetry breaking happens in the non-relativistic hydrogen atom:
there are two degenerate 1s states, one for each spin of the electron, and they are mixed by spatial rotations.
We thus can say that rotational invariance is spontaneously broken for the non-relativistic hydrogen atom.54
This example might feel contrived, and indeed it is sometimes claimed that in quantum mechanics with a
finite number of degrees of freedom obeying canonical commutation relations, with no extra label such as
spin, the ground state can’t be degenerate. To give you a flavor of these arguments, here is one in the
context of a nonrelativistic particle moving in a potential V (x) in one dimension. Say that ψ1 and ψ2 are
normalizable wave functions obeying

                                              −ψ1′′ + V (x)ψ1 (x) = Eψ1 (x)
                                              −ψ2′′ + V (x)ψ2 (x) = Eψ2 (x).                                            (13.1)

Multiplying the first equation by ψ2 and the second equation by ψ1 , taking the difference, and then integrating
we see that
                                           ψ1 ψ2′ − ψ1′ ψ2 = constant.                                    (13.2)
By normalizability ψ1 and ψ2 must go to zero at infinity, and for “reasonable” potentials ψ1′ and ψ2′ will not
grow fast enough to cancel this. The constant must therefore be zero. We can then rewrite this equation as

                                                   (log ψ1 )′ = (log ψ2 )′ ,                                            (13.3)

which we can integrate to see that ψ1 must be proportional to ψ2 and thus they are really the same state.
    On the other the other hand this argument involved various assumptions, and if we drop them then
spontaneous symmetry breaking is possible. A simple example is the quantum mechanics of a particle on a
circle x ∼ x + L with Lagrangian
                                                  mẋ2    θ
                                            L=         + ẋ.                                        (13.4)
                                                   2      L
The canonical momentum is
                                                        θ
                                               p = ẋ + ,                                           (13.5)
                                                        L
and the Hamiltonian is                                     2
                                                 1        θ
                                          H=          p−       .                                    (13.6)
                                                2m        L
The energy eigenstates are
                                                      ψn (x) = eipn x ,                                                 (13.7)
with
                                                                2πn
                                                         pn =       ,                                                   (13.8)
                                                                 L
so the energy levels are
                                                          1           2
                                                 En =        (2πn − θ) .                                                (13.9)
                                                        2mL2
  54 In real hydrogen this degeneracy is broken by the interaction between the spin of the electron and the spin of the proton,

leading to the hyperfine structure.


                                                             151
                 Figure 33: A scalar potential that exhibits spontaneous symmetry breaking.



In particular if we choose θ = π, then the n = 0 and n = 1 states are degenerate ground states. These are
actually related by a symmetry, x → −x. The Lagrangian may not look invariant under this symmetry, but
it is invariant up to a total derivative. The symmetry is manifest in the Hamiltonian formulation, as the
Hamiltonian is invariant under
                                                     x′ = −x
                                                                 2θ
                                                     p′ = −p +      .                                             (13.10)
                                                                 L
Note that this symmetry only respects the periodicity of x for θ = πm with m ∈ Z, as otherwise it does not
respect the quantization of p.55

13.2     Spontaneous symmetry breaking in quantum field theory
In the examples of spontaneous symmetry breaking in the previous subsection, the reason the ground state is
not invariant is that it still has some kind of object around, an electron in the 1s orbital in the first example
and a particle on a circle in the second, and these objects can transform nontrivially under the symmetry.
In quantum field theory however the ground state is supposed to represent the vacuum, without any objects
present, and one might expect that a state with “nothing there” should be be invariant under any global
symmetry. In fact spontaneous symmetry breaking is nonetheless possible in quantum field theory, but it
has a somewhat different character from the examples we just discussed.
    As is often the case when meeting a new phenomenon for the first time, it is best to start with a simple
example. The classic example of a quantum field theory with spontaneous symmetry breaking is ϕ4 theory
with a negative mass squared:
                                               1            m2 2 λ 4
                                        L = − ∂µ ϕ∂ µ ϕ +      ϕ − ϕ .                                     (13.11)
                                               2            2       4!
The potential for this theory is shown in figure 33; ϕ = 0 is now a local maximum rather than a global
minimum, and the true minima are at                     r
                                                           6m2
                                                  ϕ=±          .                                           (13.12)
                                                            λ
We can thus guess that this theory has two degenerate ground states |Ω, ±⟩ obeying
                                                                 r
                                                                   6m2
                                          ⟨Ω, ±|Φ(x)|Ω, ±⟩ = ±         .                                   (13.13)
                                                                    λ
  55 This breaking of a classical symmetry when θ ̸= πm is a simple example of an anomaly; we will meet other examples next

semester.


                                                           152
If this is true, then the ϕ′ = −ϕ global symmetry of the action is spontenously broken. Justifying this guess
is somewhat subtle however, and in particular it is only really correct when we study the theory in infinite
volume. To get a sense of where the subtlety comes from, we can go back to the quantum mechanics of a
particle with Hamiltonian
                                               p2     k2      λ
                                           H=      − x2 + x4 .                                        (13.14)
                                               2m     2       4!
This potential again has the form shown in figure 33, but we proved in the last section that theories of this
type have a unique ground state. So what is going on?
    We can get a better sense of what is happening by defining a pair of states |±⟩ by the property that |±⟩
minimizes
                                                  ⟨±|H|±⟩                                             (13.15)
subject to the constraint that                             r
                                                               6k 2
                                            ⟨±|x|±⟩ = ±             .                                (13.16)
                                                                λ
Were this theory to exhibit spontaneous symmetry breaking then |±⟩ would be a pair of degenerate ground
states. Let’s study their matrix elements. The x′ = −x symmetry is implemented on the Hilbert space by a
unitary operator U that commutes with H, so we have

                                 ⟨+|H|+⟩ = ⟨−|U † HU |−⟩ = ⟨−|H|−⟩ ≡ a.                              (13.17)

Moreover we have
                           ⟨+|H|−⟩ = ⟨−|U † H|−⟩ = ⟨−|HU † |−⟩ = ⟨−|H|+⟩ = b.                        (13.18)
By hermiticity we also have
                                           ⟨+|H|−⟩ = ⟨−|H|+⟩∗ ,                                      (13.19)
so b must be real. Within the subspace spanned by |±⟩ we thus have
                                                       
                                                    a b
                                             H=           ,                                          (13.20)
                                                    b a

with a and q                                                             q |+⟩ looks like a wave packet
           b both real. The real question is whether or not b = 0. The state
                2                                                               2
near x = + 6kλ , while the state |−⟩ looks like a wave packet near x = − 6kλ . We thus should expect a
nonzero tunneling amplitude between them, which in the WKB approximation is given by
                                             R x0  ′
                                                     √      ′
                                      b ∼ e− −x0 dx 2m(V (x )−E0 ) .                           (13.21)

Diagonalizing H with nonzero b shows that the true eigenstates are |+⟩ ± |−⟩, with energy a ∓ |b|. Thus the
ground state is
                                            |Ω⟩ = |+⟩ + |−⟩,                                        (13.22)
which is invariant under the symmetry so there is no spontaneous symmetry breaking.
    Let’s now apply this discussion to the quantum field theory (13.11). The key difference is that in order
to tunnel from |+⟩ to |−⟩ or vice versa, the field now needs to tunnel everywhere in space. The tunneling
exponent in the WKB approximation therefore picks up an extra factor of the volume of space. More
concretely if we define a constant mode
                                                    R d−1
                                                      d xϕ
                                              ϕ0 =
                                                    Z Vol
                                              π0 =    dd−1 xϕ̇,                                      (13.23)




                                                     153
Figure 34: Turning on an explicit symmetry breaking perturbation. This forces the system to pick |+⟩ or
|−⟩ to be the true ground state.



with π0 defined so that [ϕ0 , π0 ] = i, then the Hamiltonian derived from (13.11) becomes
                                                   π02
                                          H=             + Volume × V (ϕ0 ).                                          (13.24)
                                                 2Volume
Thus in the tunneling exponent (13.21) we should replace m → Volume and V → Volume × V , which gives
an overall factor of the spatial volume in the exponent. Thus in the infinite volume limit we have b = 0, so
there are indeed two degenerate ground states exchanged by the symmetry.56 Thus we see that spontaneous
symmetry breaking is considerably more robust in quantum field theory than it is in quantum mechanics.
    Let’s say a bit more precisely what the difference is between this example and the quantum mechanical
examples we discussed above. In the field theory model (13.11), the field ϕ is an example of what is called
an order parameter - it is a dynamical variable whose expectation value tells us which vacuum we are in.
In the hydrogen atom example we can take the order parameter to be the spin of the electron in the z-basis,
while in the example with a periodic particle it is the momentum p. In both of the single particle cases
the order parameter commutes with the Hamiltonian, as otherwise it would need to have fluctuations in the
ground state. What can happen in quantum field theory however is that we can have an order parameter
whose average over space has fluctuations in any finite volume but stops fluctuating in the limit of infinite
volume (we will see this more explicitly in the next section).

13.3     External perturbations and cluster decomposition
Given the discussion of the last subsection you might wonder why we talk about the |±⟩ states at all. If
the Hamiltonian in finite volume is really diagonal in the |+⟩ ± |−⟩ basis, shouldn’t we work in that basis
instead? What is really special about the |±⟩ states is that if we add a local perturbation to the system
which explicitly breaks the symmetry, the true ground state is close to |+⟩ or |−⟩ rather than |+⟩ + |−⟩ or
|+⟩ − |−⟩. There are various ways to think about why this criterion is important. If our quantum field theory
is describing a condensed matter system such as a magnet, then there will always be symmetry-breaking
interactions with its external environment. If we are instead doing particle physics then we can think of
ourselves as the symmetry-breaking perturbation: by existing in the universe we can’t avoid measuring the
order parameter, and the interaction between us and the fields which does this looks symmetry breaking if
(as usual in quantum mechanics) we remove ourselves from the system. Either way, the states which are
stable under external perturbations are called pointer states, and they are ones that survive in the classical
limit. It is perhaps useful to illustrate this using Schrodinger’s cat: what is special about the states |Alive⟩
and |Dead⟩ that doesn’t hold for √12 (|Alive⟩ ± |Dead⟩)? The answer is that when the cat interacts with its
 56 If the spontaneously broken symmetry is continuous then b only vanishes like a power of the volume, but it still vanishes.




                                                            154
environment, the |Alive⟩ and |Dead⟩ states are preserved while the superposition states are decohered. This
means that we ourselves always end up on a branch of the wave function where we are sure the cat is alive or
dead. Similarly when we encounter a magnet below its Curie temperature, we always see the spins aligned
in some particular direction.
    Let’s see how this works in our quantum mechanics example. Say that we introduce a small linear
perturbation to the potential,
                                                      k2      λ
                                         V (x) = gx − x2 + x4 .                                     (13.25)
                                                       2      4!
In first-order degenerate perturbation theory, the perturbed Hamiltonian is diagonal in the basis which
diagonalizes the perturbation. If we look at the matrix elements of the perturbation we have
                                                           r
                                                             6k 2
                                            ⟨±|gx|±⟩ = ±g                                           (13.26)
                                                              λ
by assumption, while the off-diagonal matrix elements of gx are much smaller since ⟨x|+⟩ and ⟨x|−⟩ are
peaked at different places. We can also see this directly from the plot of the potential as in figure 34, where
the linear perturbation breaks the degeneracy and favors one minimum or the other depending on the sign
of g. In our matrix language we are thus now diagonalizing
                                                               
                                                    a+c      b
                                            H=                                                          (13.27)
                                                      b     a−c
                             √
which has eigenvalues a ∓ b2 + c2 and eigenvectors which when |b| ≪ |c| are close to |∓⟩ if c is positive
and |±⟩ if c is negative. This idea should also be familiar in the context of the Ising model, where if we turn
on a small external magnetic field this biases the system towards all up or all down depending on the sign
of the field. In particular if the strength of the perturbation stays nonzero in the large volume limit then
eventually we will always be in the regime |b| ≪ |c| no matter how small the perturbation.
    For quantum field theory in infinite volume we can be somewhat more quantitative about the situation.
Indeed let’s say that we have a set of degenerate vacua |v⟩ where v is some set of parameters. We will assume
translation symmetry is not broken, in which case these vacua obey

                                                   P⃗ |v⟩ = 0                                            (13.28)

with P⃗ the total spatial momentum, and since there are no particles in these vacua they should be discrete
eigenvectors of P⃗ (i.e. they shouldn’t be part of some continuous set of δ-function normalized momentum
eigenstates). We can thus take each |v⟩ state to have norm one, and by choosing an orthonormal set of |v⟩
we can assume that
                                                ⟨u|v⟩ = δu,v .                                      (13.29)
Note that this is the usual Kronecker δ, even though v may include continuous parameters (in the next
subsection we will see examples where it does). Our goal is now to show that we can additionally choose
this basis so that for any local operator O(x) we have

                                         ⟨u|O(x)|v⟩ = δu,v ⟨v|O(x)|v⟩,                                   (13.30)

In other words local operators can never mix between the different vacua. This thus implies that any
deformation of the Hamiltonian by a local operator (or a sum of local operators) will be diagonal in the |v⟩
basis, and thus that these are the pointer states among which the ground state will be selected when we add
a symmetry-breaking perturbation.
    To establish (13.30), we first note that for any two local operators O1 and O2 we have

                                                          dd−1 p X
                         X                           Z
 ⟨u|O1 (⃗x)O2 (0)|v⟩ =    ⟨u|O1 (0)|w⟩⟨w|O2 (0)|v⟩ +              ⟨u|O1 (0)|N, p⃗⟩⟨N, p⃗|O2 (0)|v⟩ei⃗p·⃗x (13.31)
                         w
                                                         (2π)d−1
                                                                  N



                                                      155
where |N, p⃗⟩ are some complete set of single and multiparticle states. If we consider the limit of large |x|
the momentum integral should vanish by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma (assuming some mild integrability
of these matrix elements), so we thus have
                                                       X
                             lim ⟨u|O1 (⃗x)O2 (0)|v⟩ =   ⟨u|O1 (0)|w⟩⟨w|O2 (0)|v⟩.                    (13.32)
                           |x|→∞
                                                            w

Similarly we also have                                     X
                               lim ⟨u|O2 (0)O1 (⃗x)|v⟩ =        ⟨u|O2 (0)|w⟩⟨w|O1 (0)|v⟩.              (13.33)
                           |x|→∞
                                                            w

Now let’s assume that O1 and O2 are bosonic. They therefore must commute at spacelike separation, and
so we must have        X                             X
                          ⟨u|O1 (0)|w⟩⟨w|O2 (0)|v⟩ =   ⟨u|O2 (0)|w⟩⟨w|O1 (0)|v⟩.               (13.34)
                           w                                    w

In other words the matrices ⟨u|O1 (0)|v⟩ and ⟨u|O2 (0)|v⟩ commute for any bosonic O1 and O2 . If O1 or O2
is fermionic then its matrix elements between the |v⟩ must vanish since each |v⟩ should be invariant under
fermion parity (which is built out of infinitesimal rotations). We can therefore simultaneously diagonalize
all of the local operators, leading to (13.30).
    We can also think of these |v⟩ states as being precisely the ones where the cluster decomposition
principle is obeyed:
                                 lim ⟨v|O1 (⃗x)O2 (0)|v⟩ = ⟨v|O1 (0)|v⟩⟨v|O2 (0)|v⟩                 (13.35)
                                |x|→∞

for any O1 and O2 . One consequence of this is that the fluctuations of the zero modes of operators vanish
in these states:
                      d xO(⃗x) dd−1 yO(⃗y )
                     R d−1     R                            Z
                                                       1
                 ⟨v|                         |v⟩ =            dd−1 x⟨v|O(⃗x)O(0)|v⟩
                       Volume     Volume           Volume
                                                 = ⟨v|O(0)|v⟩⟨v|O(0)|v⟩
                                                       R d−1              R d−1
                                                         d xO(⃗x)          d yO(⃗y )
                                                 = ⟨v|             |v⟩⟨v|            |v⟩,          (13.36)
                                                          Volume            Volume
where in the first equality I used translation invariance, in the second I used that the integral is determined
by the large |x| regime since the contribution from any finite region is eliminated by the infinite volume
factor, and in the third I again used translation invariance. This shows that these |v⟩ states are indeed those
where the zero modes of the fields have definite values, and in fact usually the label v is precisely just some
list of these expectation values. In our ϕ4 theory with negative mass squared these are precisely the states
|±⟩, while in the states |+⟩ ± |−⟩ the zero mode of ϕ has O(1) fluctuations.
    It is worth mentioning that the argument for (13.30) still works if we take O to be any product of a finite
number of local operators; in the homework you will thus show that

                           ⟨u|O1 (x1 ) . . . On (xn )|v⟩ = δu,v ⟨u|O1 (x1 ) . . . On (xn )|u⟩.         (13.37)

In particular this means that states obtained by acting on distinct vacua with finitely many local operators
are always orthogonal. The Hilbert space therefore decomposes into mutually orthogonal superselection
sectors labeled by v, each of which is spanned by states of the form

                                               O1 (x1 ) . . . On (xn )|v⟩.                             (13.38)

This decomposition of the Hilbert space into superselection sectors that cannot be mixed by local operators
is the hallmark of spontaneous symmetry breaking in field theory; there is no analogous decomposition for
the quantum mechanics examples we discussed above.



                                                          156
                       Figure 35: The sombrero potential for the O(2) vector model.



13.4    Goldstone’s theorem
Spontaneous symmetry breaking is particularly interesting when the symmetry which is broken is continuous.
The classic example of this is a theory of N real scalar fields ϕi , with Lagrangian
                                                                                   !2
                                 1X µ             m2 X 2 λ              X
                             L=−     ∂ ϕi ∂µ ϕi +     ϕ −                    ϕ2i        .               (13.39)
                                 2 i              2 i i   4              i

This theory is usually called the “O(N ) vector model”, as it has an O(N ) global symmetry that rotates
among the ϕi fields:
                                               ϕ′i = Rij ϕj ,                                   (13.40)
with R ∈ O(N ). For N = 2 this potential is called the sombrero or mexican hat potential, it is shown in
figure 35. The minimum of this potential is an SN −1 in field space located at
                                                        m
                                                  |ϕ| = √ ,                                             (13.41)
                                                         λ

so to pick a vacuum we need to pick a unit vector in RN . This unit vector is preserved by an O(N − 1)
subgroup of O(N ), so this situation is typically described by saying that the O(N ) global symmetry of the
model is spontaneously broken to a O(N − 1) subgroup, with the latter still leaving the vacuum invariant.
In particular for the case of N = 2 the unbroken symmetry is just O(1) = Z2 .
    What are excitations of this theory about the ground state |n̂⟩ in which the zero mode of ϕi is pointing
in the n̂ direction? Looking at figure 35 the answer is clear: in the radial direction orthogonal to the ground
state SN −1 there is a massive scalar field excitation (with positive mass squared), while for each of the N − 1
field directions along the SN −1 there is a massless scalar field. These massless scalars are called Goldstone
bosons, or sometimes Nambu-Goldstone bosons, and their existence is the hallmark of a spontaneously
broken continuous symmetry. Note that there is an independent massless scalar for each broken generator
of O(N ). In particular for N = 2 there is one Goldstone boson. We can extract it explicitly by writing

                                                 ϕ1 = ρ cos θ
                                                 ϕ2 = ρ sin θ,                                          (13.42)

in terms of which the Lagrangian becomes

                                     1           ρ2           m2 2 λ 4
                                L = − ∂µ ρ∂ µ ρ − ∂µ θ∂ µ θ +   ρ − ρ .                                 (13.43)
                                     2           2            2    4

                                                      157
The potential causes ρ to get an expectation value
                                                              m
                                                         ρ0 = √ ,                                                    (13.44)
                                                               λ
so writing
                                                        ρ = ρ0 + δ                                                   (13.45)
we have
                                1                   (ρ0 + δ)2                      λ
                        L = − ∂µ δ∂ µ δ − m2 δ 2 −            ∂µ θ∂ µ θ + λρ0 δ 3 + δ 4           (13.46)
                                2                       2                          4
                                                   √
we can see that δ is a massive scalar field of mass 2m and θ is our massless Goldstone boson.
   You may wonder if these massless bosons were an artifact of this model, or perhaps if they can acquire
a mass due to loop corrections. The answer to both questions is no, as we can learn from Goldstone’s
theorem:57
     In a relativistic quantum field theory with an internal global symmetry group G that is spontaneously
      broken to some subgroup H, for each broken direction in G there is an exactly massless scalar particle.

There are various approaches to proving Goldstone’s theorem, my favorite is the following. Let Jaµ be the
Noether currents for a continuous internal global symmetry group G that is spontaneously broken in some
vacuum state |v⟩, and let On be some set of local operators that transform nontrivially under the symmetry:
                                                    X
                                            δS On =     Tnm Om .                                     (13.47)
                                                               m

The Ward identity that we proved last semester shows that we have
                                 ∂
                                    ⟨v|T Jaµ (x)On (0)|v⟩ = iδ d (x)⟨v|δS On (0)|v⟩ + . . . ,                        (13.48)
                                ∂xµ
where . . . indicates possible Schwinger terms involving derivatives of the δ-function. Defining the Fourier
transform
                                                        dd p
                                                     Z
                             ⟨v|T Jaµ (x)On (0)|v⟩ =         ⟨v|T Jaµ (p)On (0)|v⟩eip·x ,            (13.49)
                                                       (2π)d
we can rewrite (13.48) as
                                     pµ ⟨v|T Jaµ (p)On (0)|v⟩ = ⟨v|δS On (0)|v⟩ + . . . ,                            (13.50)
where the . . . terms come from Schwinger terms and are proportional to positive powers of momentum (from
the derivatives on the δ function). If G is spontaneously broken then the quantity on the right-hand side
cannot be zero, since                               X
                                  ⟨v|δS On (0)|v⟩ =   Tnm ⟨v|Om (0)|v⟩ ̸= 0,                       (13.51)
                                                           m

is precisely the condition that the vacuum transforms nontrivially under the symmetry generated by Jaµ
(assuming that we choose On to include any fields which have a vacuum expectation value).
    Now let’s consider the limit of zero momentum. To be consistent with (13.50) the two point function in
momentum space must have a nonvanishing term
                                                                                   pµ
                                      ⟨v|T Jaµ (p)On (0)|v⟩ ⊃ ⟨v|δS On (0)|v⟩ ×       .                              (13.52)
                                                                                   p2
  57 This is not the most general version of the theorem. There is also a non-relativistic version of Goldstone’s theorem, and

the spontaneous breaking of spacetime symmetries such as translation and rotation invariance also leads to Goldstone bosons.
It is only for relativistic internal symmetries however that the number of Goldstone bosons must equal the number of broken
symmetry directions.




                                                            158
Therefore the two-point function must have a pole at zero momentum, which I’ll remind you is equivalent
to saying there is a massless particle which can be created by O(0) and annihilated by Jaµ . Moreover the
momentum pµ can be recognized as the intertwiner which allows a vector field Jaµ to annihilate a particle of
zero spin (think ∂ µ ϕ in free field theory), so our massless particle has spin zero. This particle must have the
same internal symmetry charges as Jaµ since it is annihilated by it.
    We will now count the number of Goldstone bosons. We will restrict the currents Jaµ to a basis that spans
only the broken subspace, since the unbroken currents do not have a pole in the two point function. Thus
for the rest of this paragraph a runs only over the number N of broken generators. Let b run over the set of
Goldstone bosons contributing to this two-point function. The relevant matrix elements are determined by
Lorentz invariance to be
                                                                     iFab pµ
                                                  ⟨v|Jaµ (0)|⃗
                                                             p, b⟩ = p
                                                                       2ωp⃗
                                                                      Zbn
                                                  ⟨⃗
                                                   p, b|On (0)|v⟩ = p       ,                                         (13.53)
                                                                       2ωp⃗

and from the two-point function we have58
                                   X            X
                                                   a
                                      Fab Zbn =   Tnm ⟨v|Om (0)|v⟩.                                                   (13.54)
                                             b               m

We can view the right hand side as a matrix T an , which by definition has a rank which is equal to N since
                                    a
this is the number of generators Tnm   such that no linear combination of them leaves the vacuum invariant.
The left-hand side is a product of two matrices, so since their product has rank N they must each separately
have rank at least N . The rank of Fab is at most N , so we see that indeed there are N linearly-independent
Goldstone bosons that can be annihilated/created by Jaµ .
    There are many examples of Goldstone bosons in nature, here are a few:
     In QCD in the limit mu = md = 0 there is an SU (2) × SU (2) flavor symmetry. This symmetry turns
      out to be spontaneously broken to SU (2), leading to three Goldstone bosons: the charged pions π±
      and the neutral pion π0 .

     In superfluid liquid helium particle number symmetry is spontaneously broken, which leads to a mass-
      less Goldstone boson whose dynamics account for the remarkable bulk properties of the system.
     In a crystal spatial translation symmetry is spontaneously broken, which leads to Goldstone bosons
      called phonons.

     In the early universe it seems that there was an approximate spontaneously broken shift symmetry of
      some field called the inflaton, whose approximate Goldstone boson fluctuations eventually gave rise to
      the anisotropy of the cosmic microwave background and the stars and galaxies we see today.

13.5      The Abelian Higgs model
What happens if a gauge symmetry is spontaneously broken? This turns out to be a rather slippery question
to make precise, and in particular to the extent that spontaneous breaking of a gauge symmetry makes sense
it does not lead to any vacuum degeneracy.59 It is thus safest not to refer to (or think of) the phenomenon
as spontaneous symmetry breaking; I’ll instead refer to it as the Higgs mechanism.60 Rather than give a
  58 This follows from inserting (13.53) into equation 9.16 from QFT I and then using 9.63 near the pole.
  59 This has been shown rigorously from a lattice point of view, where it is called “Elitzur’s theorem”.
  60 The history of the Higgs mechanism is a convoluted mess, with the list of people deserving some credit including Anderson,

Brout, Englert, Guralnik, Hagen, Higgs, Kibble, and Nambu. I’m not enough of a historian to take sides, but it does need a
name and Higgs is a fine one.




                                                              159
general discussion we’ll instead just consider a simple example, the Abelian Higgs model, which is another
name for scalar QED with a negative mass squared:
                                    1                                   λ
                               L = − F µν Fµν − (Dµ ϕ)∗ Dµ ϕ + m2 |ϕ|2 − |ϕ|4 .                        (13.55)
                                    4                                   4
Here we take ϕ to have charge q, so the covariant derivative is

                                            Dµ ϕ = (∂µ − iqAµ ) ϕ.                                     (13.56)

We can guess that the potential causes ϕ to get a nonzero expectation value, but since ϕ is not gauge-invariant
we need to be more careful about analyzing what this theory does. It turns out the most useful approach is
to introduce the field redefinition
                                              ϕ(x) = ρ(x)eiqθ(x) ,                                      (13.57)
with ρ > 0. This field redefinition is not good near ρ = 0, but at least to all orders in perturbation theory
we are ok as long as we end up expanding around somewhere other than ρ = 0. In terms of these variables
the gauge transformations of this theory are

                                               A′µ = Aµ + ∂µ Ω
                                                θ′ = θ + Ω
                                                ρ′ = ρ.                                                (13.58)

Substituting this redefinition into the covariant derivative we have

                                      Dµ ϕ = (∂µ ρ − iq(Aµ − ∂µ θ)ρ)eiqθ ,                             (13.59)

so the Lagrangian becomes
                       1                              λ
                  L = − Fµν F µν − ∂µ ρ∂ µ ρ + m2 ρ2 − ρ4 − q 2 ρ2 (Aµ − ∂µ θ) (Aµ − ∂ µ θ) .          (13.60)
                       4                              4
The potential thus sets ρ to                            r
                                                        2m2
                                                 ρ0 =       .                                       (13.61)
                                                         λ
I emphasize that ρ is gauge-invariant, so this expectation value does not break the gauge symmetry; the
vacuum is gauge-invariant (as it had better be since it should be a physical state). The Lagrangian (13.60)
has a number of remarkable features, which we will now discuss in turn:
    The variable θ appears only in derivatives, so one might naively expect it to lead to a massless scalar
     particle. If we remove the photon from the theory then this is indeed the case, with θ being the
     Goldstone boson for the spontaneous breaking of U (1) global charge rotation symmetry. In fact this is
     just the O(2) vector model written in slightly different variables. In the theory (13.60) however, we can
     remove the θ field by doing the gauge transformation Ω = −θ. This is called going to unitarity gauge,
     which has gauge-fixing condition θ = 0. The theory (in any gauge) thus has no physical Goldstone
     boson, it has been removed by the gauge redundancy. This is consistent with the general slogan that
     the Higgs mechanism is not the same thing as spontaneous symmetry breaking.
    If we look at fluctuations of the ρ field, which we can parametrize as ρ = ρ0 + √δ2 , the kinetic term for
     δ has the form
                                                     1
                                               L ⊃ − ∂µ δ∂ µ δ − m2 δ 2 ,                               (13.62)
                                                     2
                                                                            √
     so this theory has a massive scalar excitation with mass mHiggs = 2m. This is called the Higgs
     boson, and a close analogue of it was discovered in 2012 by the ATLAS and CMS experiments at the
     Large Hadron Collider in Geneva.


                                                        160
    The quadratic action for the photon now includes a mass term
                                                                  µ2
                                                        J ⊃−         Aµ Aµ ,                                        (13.63)
                                                                  2
       with the photon mass µ being given by61
                                                           √          2qm
                                                      µ=       2qρ0 = √ .                                           (13.66)
                                                                        λ
       This term does not look gauge-invariant, but the full Lagrangian is; the Higgs mechanism thus gives us
       a gauge-invariant way to have a massive photon. More concretely the gauge-invariant massive photon
                eµ = Aµ − ∂µ θ. This situation is sometimes described by saying that the massive photon has
       field is A
       “eaten the Goldstone boson”. Of course in the standard model the true photon is massless, but an
       analogue of this mechanism leads to three heavy massive particles of spin one called the W+ , W− , and
       Z bosons.
    In the standard model the Higgs mechanism gives mass to matter fields in addition to gauge bosons.
     Here is how it works. Say we have two charged spinors Ψ1 and Ψ2 , with charges q1 and q2 respectively,
     and moreover let’s assume that q1 + q = q2 . Then we can have a gauge invariant Yukawa-type term in
     the Lagrangian
                                             L ⊃ −igϕψ 2 ψ1 + c.c.                                 (13.67)
       If ϕ gets an expectation value then this becomes a mass term that mixes ψ1 and ψ2 , and we can
       rediagonalize the spinor basis to find a pair of massive spinors.

13.6     Superconductivity
The first physics application of the Higgs mechanism was not actually to particle physics; it was originally
proposed (before the work of Higgs) by Nambu and then Anderson as a model of superconductivity. In
this context it is therefore sometimes called the Anderson-Higgs mechanism, especially by condensed matter
physicists, although I don’t think that is fair to Nambu. In any event the key point is that a superconductor
is really nothing but a material in which some low-energy field with electric charge q has a radial part ρ
which gets an expectation value. In other words it is a material in which electromagnetism is Higgsed!
We won’t concern ourselves here with why this happens, for that we need the BCS theory for conventional
superconductors and something fancier for unconventional superconductors, but given this definition we can
easily understand the most famous superconducting phenomona without any need for complicated models.62
    The idea is that inside a superconducting material we can describe the low-energy behavior using the
photon field Aµ and the field θ corresponding to the phase of the operator whose radial part gets an expecta-
tion value (meaning that the phase is eiqθ where q is the charge of the operator). In order for the low-energy
action to be gauge-invariant, it can depend on Aµ and θ only through the combination Aµ − ∂µ θ. Moreover
we’d like the system to be stable when A and θ both vanish, so the energy should have a minimum when
this quantity vanishes. These expectations are realized for the Abelian Higgs Lagrangian (13.60), but we
do not need to to use the details of this Lagrangian (and in fact it is only a good approximation near the
transition temperature, where it is referred to as the Landau-Ginzburg theory). In general if we are deep
inside a superconductor which is in (or near) its ground state we should expect that

                                                        Aµ = ∂µ θ.                                                  (13.68)
 61 To see that this term indeed has the interpretation of a photon mass, we can look at how it modifies Maxwell’s equations.

These become
                                                       ∂ µ Fµν = µ2 Aν ,                                              (13.64)
which in Lorenz gauge ∂µ Aµ = 0 have the form
                                                       ∂ 2 Aν = µ2 Aν .                                               (13.65)
Thus the solutions in momentum space obey p2 = −µ2 .
 62 We here only give a rather cursory treatment, see section 21.6 of Weinberg for more.




                                                            161
Figure 36: Magnetic flux through a superconducting loop in the presence of a persistent current I: the flux
through the disk bounded by the dashed line is quantized in units of 2π/q, so the current cannot decay. The
                                                              ⃗ and J⃗ vanish.
current is flowing at the edge of the loop, inside of it both B



Since this is pure gauge, the electric and magnetic fields must both vanish. The vanishing of the electric field
is familiar for a perfect conductor, the vanishing of the magnetic field inside of a superconductor is called
the Meissner effect.63
    Of course the most famous feature of a superconductor is electrical conductivity with zero resistance. To
understand this, we first need to appreciate that since qθ is a phase, θ is a periodic variable obeying
                                                                   2πn
                                                       θ∼θ+            .                                              (13.69)
                                                                    q
Now let’s consider a closed loop of superconducting material. Consider circular path L which circles the
loop once within the material, which we can view as the boundary of a disk D that extends outside of the
superconducting material. See figure 36 for an illustration. We can compute the magnetic flux through D
via the following calculation:
                    Z            Z            Z
                        ⃗    ⃗
                        B · dA =    ⃗
                                    A · d⃗x =    ⃗ · d⃗x = θ(2π) − θ(0) = 2πn
                                                 ∇θ                            n ∈ Z.             (13.70)
                      D           L            L                           q
Thus the magnetic flux through the loop is quantized, with the quantization integer being set by the number
of times that θ winds around the loop. Since this flux is linearly proportional to the current circulating
around the loop (see e.g. the Biot-Savart law), this means the current is also quantized. In particular this
means that the current cannot decay continuously, so if it starts with n ̸= 0 then it must remain so. In other
words a superconducting loop can support a persistent current that lasts forever without any external
potential applied, which is the hallmark of superconductivity! Experimentally the set of allowed currents
can be measured and gives q = −2e, so apparently in a typical superconductor the operator whose radial
component gets an expectation value has the charge of two electrons - the excitations it annihilates are
typically called Cooper pairs.
    It is interesting to consider where the persistent current is located within the current loop. Inside the loop
we have B  ⃗ = 0 by the Meissner effect, and so by Ampere’s law we have (assuming the current is stationary)
J⃗ = ⃗
     ∇×B    ⃗ = 0. So there is no current flowing inside the loop. It therefore must all be flowing right at the
surface! This is a typical feature of superconductors - most of the interesting physics has to do with what
   63 Incidentally this already shows that there is more to a superconductor than just being a perfect conductor - the magnetic

field inside the latter has to be constant, but it doesn’t have to be zero.


                                                             162
happens at an interface between a superconductor and a material (such as air) in which electromagnetic
gauge symmetry is not Higgsed. Another example of such a phenomenon is the Josephson effect, where
bringing two superconductors which are at different potentials close together results in an oscillating current
between them. The Josephson effect has many applications, for example in SQUIDs (which are very precise
magnetometers) and as qubits for potential quantum computing architectures.




                                                     163
13.7      Problems
   1. In this problem we will learn a bit more about pointer states. We will consider a model with two
      qubits, with the first qubit being the “system” that we are keeping track of and the second qubit
      being an “environment” that we are not keeping track of. We will take the dynamics to be a unitary
      transformation that acts as64
                                               U |i, j⟩ = |i, j + i⟩,                            (13.71)
       where i, j = 0, 1 and the addition is mod 2. In this model there is no “internal” Hamiltonian for
       the system alone, we only have the interaction (13.71) with the environment. We can interpret this
       interaction as the environment “measuring” the system qubit in the z basis. We are interested in
       understanding what this dynamics looks like from the point of view of the system alone, starting from
       a product state
                                                  ρ = ρS ⊗ ρE .                                       (13.72)
       We can thus compute the evolution of the system as

                                                  ρ′S = TrE U (ρS ⊗ ρE ) U † .
                                                                            
                                                                                                                      (13.73)

       This kind of map from ρS to ρS ′ is called a quantum channel, and it is a generalization of unitary
       time evolution to an open quantum system. Note that the evolution map depends on the environment
       state ρE , as it must when the two systems are interacting. Denoting the channel as C, we can define
       it on any operator XS as:
                                         C(XS ) ≡ TrE U (XS ⊗ ρE ) U † .
                                                                       
                                                                                                    (13.74)
       (a) Show that for any ρE which is diagonal in the |0⟩, |1⟩ basis, we have

                                                          C(|i⟩⟨j|) = δij |i⟩⟨i|.                                     (13.75)

            This shows that for any diagonal ρE , the basis states |0⟩ and |1⟩ are pointer states for the system
            S. In particular this is true if we take the state on E to be maximally uncertain, ρE = IE /2,
            which is a plausible initial state for an environment that we do not control.
       (b) Now compute the action of C on the initial state ρS = |ψ⟩⟨ψ|, with |ψ⟩ = √12 (|0⟩ + |1⟩). What you
           will find is called decoherence, and it is the mechanism by which classical mechanics emerges
           from quantum mechanics.
       The idea here is that the |0⟩ and |1⟩ pointer states are like the |±⟩ states of a quantum system with a
       spontaneously broken Z2 symmetry.
   2. Repeat the argument for (13.30) to show that (13.37) also holds in the same basis.
   3. Consider a scalar field theory where ϕ is an N × N matrix of scalar fields and the Lagrangian is

4. In this problem we will discuss the Feynman rules for the abelian Higgs model. For this purpose it is
   easier not to use a radial parametrization of ϕ, instead we will take
                                                     1
                                           ϕ = ϕ0 + √ (ζ + iχ) ,                                    (13.78)
                                                      2
  taking ϕ0 > 0 and ζ and χ both real.
   (a) Write out the Abelian Higgs Lagrangian in terms of Aµ , ζ, and χ. Note in particular that there
       is a quadratic term mixing Aµ and χ.
   (b) Unitarity gauge in this model is not good in perturbation theory, as it makes the photon propagator
       unpleasant. It is better to choose the gauge fixing condition to cancel the cross term you found
       in the previous problem. Show that this is accomplished by a gauge-fixing term

                                                  2ξ
       and argue that the Faddeev-Poppov determinant for these gauge fixing is field-independent and
       thus can be ignored.
   (c) In this gauge what are the propagators for the Aµ , ζ, and ζ fields as a function of ξ?
   (d) Draw all of the interaction vertices for this theory. You do not need to work out the vertex factors,
